. At each time step, it creates copies of the environment and run each expert policy on these copies, and then it selects the action of the expert policy with the highest return. For simplicity, we refer to this algorithm as MAMBA. Note that MAMBA assumes that one has access to the underlying reward function. Thus this baseline is using significantly more information than our approach.

\bullet

Best expert.
We also compared our algorithm with the best expert policy.

Figure 3:
Learning curves of return with respect to the number of queries for different algorithms and numbers of experts.

The main results are shown in Figure
3
. We first noticed that our algorithm outperforms passive learning with noisy experts in all settings. Moreover, we beat the noiseless version when there is only one expert. Intuitively, getting feedback from noiseless experts is a very strong assumption and it is not surprising to see that the performance is improved with this stronger feedback. Note that our algorithm is only getting noisy labels as feedback. We also note that, despite the fact that MAMBA achieves better results than the best expert policy (in terms of the value function), it is still worse than our algorithm. Indeed, MAMBA does not even learn a policy that can solve the task when
M\geq 2
. This is because by our construction of experts, there is no single expert that is capable of solving the task alone. Note that MAMBA performs well in the one expert case because in that case, the (single) expert can reliably solve the control task.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, or goal is to develop algorithms for online IL with active queries with small regret and query complexity bounds. Towards that end, we started by considering the selective sampling setting (IL with
H=1
), and provided a selective sampling algorithm that can work with general function classes
\mathcal{F}
and modeling assumptions, and relies on access to an online regression oracle w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}
to make its predictions (Section
3
). The provided regret and query complexity bounds depend on the margin of the expert model. We then extended our selective sampling algorithm to interactive IL (Section
4
). For IL, we showed that the margin term that appears in the regret and the query complexity depends on the margin of the expert on counterfactual trajectories that would have been observed on following the expert policy (that we wish to compare to), instead of the trajectories that the learner observes. Thus, if the expert always chooses actions that leads to states where it is confident (i.e. has less margin), the margin term will be smaller. We also considered extensions to bandit feedback, and learning with multiple experts.

We conclude with a discussion of future research directions:

\bullet

Computationally efficient algorithms:
The algorithms that we considered in this paper are not computationally efficient beyond simple function classes (linear functions). In particular, our algorithms need to perform minimization over
\mathcal{F}
which could be NP-hard when
\mathcal{F}
is non-convex. Furthermore, even when minimization over
\mathcal{F}
is tractable, our algorithms need to maintain a version space of feasible functions in
\mathcal{F}
(e.g. in
(
5
)
or
(
10
)
) in order to compute the query condition, which is also intractable without strong assumptions on
\mathcal{F}
.

\bullet

Learning via offline regression oracles
: The algorithms that we considered in this paper rely on access to
online
regression oracles w.r.t. the underlying function class. While we have rigorous understanding of online algorithms for various classical settings e.g. linear functions, etc., provably scaling these algorithms for more complex function classes used in practice, e.g. neural networks, is still an active area of research. On the other hand, the theory and practice of offline regression w.r.t. these complex function classes is much more developed. Towards that end, it would be interesting to explore if our algorithms can be generalized to work with offline regression oracles. A potential approach is to rely on the epoching trick used in Algorithm
4
and fitting a fresh model
\widehat{f}_{e}
via offline regression at the beginning of each epoch, and then using it to predict
\widehat{y}
for all time steps in epoch
e
. However, this would result in a dependence on the eluder dimension in both the regret and the query complexity. Improving this is an interesting future research direction.

\bullet

IL with bandit feedback:
In Section
3.3
, we considered selective sampling with bandit feedback—where the learner only receives feedback on whether its chosen action matches the action of the noisy expert. Extending the framework of learning with bandit feedback to imitation learning, and for multiple experts, is an interesting direction for future research. Practically speaking, IL with bandit feedback has tremendous applications from online advertising to robotics.

\bullet

Extension to unknown
T
:
The algorithms rely on the knowledge of
T
to set the query condition and the constraint set. Extending our algorithms to operate without a-priori knowledge of
T
, e.g. by extending the standard doubling trick in interactive learning for our setting, is an interesting technical direction.

Appendix A
Further Discussion on Related Works

Selective Sampling.

There is a large bank of both theoretical and empirical work for active learning and selective sampling. Perhaps the work closest to ours is the work of
Zhu and Nowak 2022
. In this paper, the authors consider binary classification problem and provide bounds on number of queries and bound on excess risk in the active learning framework. Their algorithm also relies on regression oracle. However, there are many key differences: Firstly, their guarantees for regret for selective sampling problem (see for instance Theorem 10 on page 28 of
Zhu and Nowak 2022
) has a dependence on disagreement coefficient in the regret bound as well as number of queries. On the contrary, as we show in our work, one only needs to pay for eluder dimension or disagreement coefficient in query complexity and not in regret bound. Furthermore, we supplement our result with lower bound showing that unless one has label complexity that depends on star number (and hence can be also related to worst case disagreement coefficient), one can not get a small enough regret bound. So the separation between regret bound (that is independent of eluder dimension/star number/disagreement coefficient) and query complexity (that depends on those quantities) is real. Secondly, the results in
(
Zhu and Nowak 2022
)
dont automatically adapt to the margin region and in general there is no way to estimate the parameters of Tsybakov’s noise condition. Finally, their regret bounds depend on pseudo dimension and are thus generally suboptimal for complex
\mathcal{F}
.

Imitation Learning.

IL has enjoyed tremendous research from both theoretical and empirical perspective in the last decade; notable references include
Ross et al. 2011
;
Ross and Bagnell 2014
;
Sun et al. 2017
;
Chang et al. 2015
;
Brantley et al. 2019
;
Brantley et al. 2020
;
Nguyen and Daumé III 2020
.
Ross et al. 2011
initiated research on using online regression oracles to model the expert feedback, and provided regret bounds for IL. The key differences between our work and prior theoretical works on IL are as follows: Firstly, we consider active querying, and provide query complexity bounds for our algorithms. Secondly, and more importantly, we consider interactive IL with noisy expert feedback whereas prior works was restricted to exact expert feedback. Finally, our regret and query complexity bounds scale with the number of times when the comparator policy (induced by the expert) goes to the states where expert has a small margin (instead of the number of times when the learner goes to such states). In many cases, the margin error term corresponding to the comparator policy could be much smaller. On the empirical side, there is a long line of research that provided algorithms and empirical heuristics for making IL sample efficient by modeling the experts in both single expert and multiple expert settings
(
Beliaev et al. 2022
;
Cao et al. 2022
;
Hejna and Sadigh 2023
;
Du et al. 2023
;
Hao et al. 2022
)
; however most of these algorithm do not come with any rigorous guarantees.

Appendix B
Useful Tools and Notation

Additional notation.

Throughout the paper, we assume that the ties are broken arbitrarily but consistently. Vector-valued variables are denoted with small alphabets like
u,v
, etc, and matrix-valued variables are denoted with capital alphabets like
F,G
, etc. For any two distributions
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, we define
\mathrm{KL}{\left(D_{1}\|D_{2}\right)}
to denote the KL divergence between
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. Furthermore,
\mathrm{kl}{\left(b_{1}\|b_{2}\right)}
denotes the KL divergence between
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(b_{1})
and
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(b_{2})
. Finally, we assume that
\|f(x)\|\leq B\leq 1
for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

The following lemma is used throughout the appendix, and its proof is trivial.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be any two events such that
\mathcal{E}_{1}\implies\mathcal{E}_{2}
then
\mathbf{1}{\{\mathcal{E}_{1}\}}\leq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{2}\}
.

B.1
Basic Probabilistic Tools

Lemma 2

(Theorem 1 in
Srebro et al. 2010
)
.

Let
T>0
, and let
\mathcal{F}=\{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\}
be an arbitrary function class, and
\ell
be an
\gamma
-smooth and non-negative loss such that
\left\lvert\ell(f(x),y)\right\rvert\leq B
for all
x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y},f\in\mathcal{F}
. For any
\delta>0
, we have with probability at least
1-\delta
over a random sample of size
T
, for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle T\En_{(x,y)\sim\mu}\left[\ell\left(f(x),y\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}\ell\left(f(x_{t}),y_{t}\right)+c_{1}\left(HT\log^{3}(T)\mathsf{Rad}^{2}_{T}(\mathcal{F})+B\log(1/\delta)\right)

where
c_{1}<10^{5}
is a numeric constant, and
\mathsf{Rad}_{T}(\mathcal{F})
denotes the Rademacher complexity of the class
\mathcal{F}
.

The precise value of the numeric constant
c_{1}
in the above can be derived from
Srebro et al. 2010
and
Mendelson 2002
. Note that for finite function classes, we have
\mathsf{Rad}_{T}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert)}}{{T}}}\right)
and thus the second term above is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\log(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert)\right)
. In general, we have that
T\mathsf{Rad}^{2}_{T}(\mathcal{F})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{sq}}(\mathcal{F};T)}\right)

(
Rakhlin and Sridharan 2014
)
, and thus the second term is always dominated by the other terms in our regret and query complexity bounds.

The following inequalities are well-known; we use the version stated in
Zhu and Nowak 2022
.

Lemma 3

(Freedman’s inequality)
.

Let
\left\{X_{t}\right\}_{t\leq T}
be a real-valued martingale different sequence adapted to the filtration
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
, and let
\En_{t}\left[\cdot\right]\vcentcolon={}\En\left[\cdot\mid\mathfrak{F}_{t-1}\right]
. If
\lvert X_{t}\rvert\leq B
almost surely, then for any
\eta\in(0,1/B)
, the following holds with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}\leq\eta\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En_{t}[X_{t}^{2}]+\frac{B\log(1/\delta)}{\eta}.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\{X_{t}\}_{t\leq T}
be a sequence of positive valued random variables adapted to the filtration
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
, and and let
\En_{t}\left[\cdot\right]\vcentcolon={}\En\left[\cdot\mid\mathfrak{F}_{t-1}\right]
. If
X_{t}\leq B
almost surely, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En_{t}\left[X_{t}\right]+4B\log(2/\delta),

and

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En_{t}\left[X_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}+8B\log(2/\delta).

B.2
Online Learning

Lemma 5
.

Suppose that the labels are generated according to the
(
2
)
where the link function satisfies Assumption
1
. Additionally, assume that the regression oracle satisfies the guarantee
(
3
)
. Then, for any
\delta\leq 1/e
and
T\geq 3
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have for all
t\leq T
,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\|f_{s}(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}\vcentcolon={}\frac{4}{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{\ell_{\phi}}}(\mathcal{F};T)}+\frac{112}{\lambda^{2}}\log(4\log^{2}(T)/\delta),

where
B
is defined such that
\sup_{x}f(x)\leq B
.

Proof.

Using
Agarwal 2013
along with an Union bound implies that for all
t\leq T
,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\|f_{s}(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\lambda}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left(\ell_{\phi}(f_{s}(x_{s}),y_{s})-\ell_{\phi}({f^{\star}}(x_{s}),y_{s})\right)+\frac{112}{\lambda^{2}}\log(4\log^{2}(T)/\delta).

Plugging in the regret bound
(
3
)
in the above, we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\|f_{s}(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{T}\|f_{s}(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\|^{2}\leq\frac{4}{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{\ell_{\phi}}}(\mathcal{F};T)}+\frac{112}{\lambda^{2}}\log(4\log^{2}(T)/\delta).

∎

B.3
Eluder Dimension, Disagreement Coefficient, and Star Number

For the sake of completeness, we recall the scalar versions of scale-sensitive eluder dimension, and disagreement coefficient introduced in
Russo and Van Roy 2013
;
Foster et al. 2020
, which is defined for a class
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
of scalar valued functions.

Definition 4

(Scale-sensitive eluder dimension (scalar version),
Russo and Van Roy 2013
;
Foster et al. 2020
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
. Fix any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
, and define
\mathfrak{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})
to be the length of the longest sequence of contexts
x_{1},x_{2},\dots x_{m}
such that for all
i
, there exists
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\displaystyle\lvert f_{i}(x_{i})-{f^{\star}}(x_{i})\rvert>\beta,\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j<i}(f_{i}(x_{j})-{f^{\star}}(x_{j}))^{2}\leq\beta^{2}.

We define the scale-sensitive eluder dimension as
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta_{0};{f^{\star}})\vcentcolon={}\sup_{\beta_{0}\geq\beta}\mathfrak{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})
.

We next provide some examples of function classes with bounded eluder dimension. The examples
(a)-(c)
first appeared in
Russo and Van Roy 2013
, and
(d)
first appeared in
Osband and Van Roy 2014
.

(a)

For any function class
\mathcal{F}:\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
and
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
,
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta_{0};{f^{\star}})\leq O(\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert)
.

(b)

For the class
\mathcal{F}
of linear functions on a known feature map
\phi
i.e. ,
\mathcal{F}=\{f\mid f(x)=\langle\theta_{f},\phi(x)\rangle\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \theta_{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\theta_{f}\|\leq 1\}
, we have
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta_{0};{f^{\star}})\leq O(d\log(1/\varepsilon))
.

(c)

For the class
\mathcal{F}
of generalized linear functions on a known feature map
\phi
i.e. ,
\mathcal{F}=\{f\mid f(x)=g(\langle\theta_{f},\phi(x)\rangle)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \theta_{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\theta_{f}\|\leq 1\}
where
g
is an increasing continuously differentiable function, we have
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta_{0};{f^{\star}})\leq O(dr^{2}\log(L/\varepsilon))
where
r=\nicefrac{{\sup_{\theta,x}g^{\prime}(\langle\theta,x\rangle)}}{{\inf_{\theta,x}g^{\prime}(\langle\theta,x\rangle)}}
and
L=\sup_{\theta,x}g^{\prime}(\langle\theta,\phi(x)\rangle)
.

(d)

For the class
\mathcal{F}
of quadratic functions on a known feature map
\phi
i.e.
\mathcal{F}=\{f\mid f(x)=\phi(x)^{\top}\Sigma_{f}\phi(x)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \Sigma_{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\|\Sigma_{f}\|_{F}\leq 1\}
.

We next recall the definition of scale-sensitive disaggrement coefficient which appears in our bounds for the case of stochastic contexts.

Definition 5

(Scale-sensitive disagreement coefficient (scalar version),
Foster et al. 2020
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
. For any
f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}
, and
\gamma_{0},\varepsilon_{0}>0
, the value function disagreement coefficient
\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon_{0},\gamma_{0};f^{\star}\right)
is defined as

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu}\sup_{\gamma>\gamma_{0},\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{0}}\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\cdot\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\mid\left\lvert f(x)-f^{\star}(x)\right\rvert>\varepsilon,\|f-f^{\star}\|_{\mu}\leq\gamma\right)\right\}\vee 1

where
\|f\|=\sqrt{\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[f^{2}(x)\right]}
.

As we will show in Lemma
6
below, the scale-sensitive disagreement coefficient of
\mathcal{F}
is always bounded by the eluder dimension of
\mathcal{F}
upto a constant factor on the dependence on
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
. However, the disagreement coefficient can be significantly smaller than the eluder dimension because it can leverage additional distributional structure. We refer the reader to
Foster et al. 2020
for bounds on the eluder dimension, and the disagreement coefficient for various function classes. In the following, we extend the above definitions to vector-valued functions to account for the vector-valued function classes that we consider in this work.

Definition 6

(Scale-sensitive eluder dimension (normed version))
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{K}\}
. Fix any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
, and define
\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})
to be the length of the longest sequence of contexts
x_{1},x_{2},\dots x_{m}
such that for all
i
, there exists
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\displaystyle\|f_{i}(x_{i})-{f^{\star}}(x_{i})\|>\beta,\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j<i}\|f_{i}(x_{j})-{f^{\star}}(x_{j})\|^{2}\leq\beta^{2}.

We define the scale-sensitive eluder dimension as
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta^{\prime};{f^{\star}})=\sup_{\beta\geq\beta^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})
.

We note that the normed eluder dimension can be lower bounded in terms of the eluder dimension of scalar-valued function class obtained by projecting the output of the functions in
\mathcal{F}
along different coordinates. Let
\mathcal{F}_{j}=\{P_{j}f\mid P_{j}f(x)=f(x)[j],\penalty\ f\in\mathcal{F}\}
, then clearly, for any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
,
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})\geq\sup_{j\in[d]}\mathfrak{E}(P_{j}\mathcal{F},\beta;P_{j}{f^{\star}})
. Furthermore, we always have that
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})\leq\kappa\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathfrak{E}(P_{j}\mathcal{F},\beta;P_{j}{f^{\star}})
, where
\kappa
hides
\mathrm{poly}(d)
factors. We next define the normed version of disagreement coefficient for vector-valued functions.

Definition 7

(Scale sensitive disagreement coefficient (normed version),
Foster et al. 2020
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{K}\}
.
For any
f^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}
, and
\beta_{0},\varepsilon_{0}>0
, the value function disagreement coefficient
\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0};f^{\star}\right)
is defined as

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu}\sup_{\beta>\beta_{0},\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{0}}\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\cdot\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\mid\left\|f(x)-f^{\star}(x)\right\|>\varepsilon,\|f-f^{\star}\|_{\mu}\leq\beta\right)\right\}\vee 1

where
\|f\|_{\mu}=\sqrt{\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[\|f(x)\|^{2}\right]}
.

We additionally also define the following bivariate version of eluder dimension for vector-valued functions.

Definition 8

(Scale-sensitive eluder dimension (bivariate version))
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{K}\}
.
Fix any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
, and define
\check{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})
to be the length of the longest sequence of contexts and actions
(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\dots(x_{m},y_{m})
such that for all
i
, there exists
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\displaystyle\lvert f_{i}(x_{i})[y_{i}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{i})[y_{i}]\rvert>\beta,\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j<i}(f_{i}(x_{j})[y_{j}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{j})[y_{j}])^{2}\leq\beta^{2}.

We define the scale sensitive eluder dimension (mixed version) as
\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})\vcentcolon={}\sup_{\beta\geq\beta_{0}}\check{\mathfrak{E}}^{\prime}(\mathcal{F},\beta_{0};{f^{\star}})
.

We next define the strong variant of scale-sensitive star number.

Definition 9

(scale-sensitive star number (strong version),
Foster et al. 2020
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{K}\}
. For any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\beta>0
, let
{\mathfrak{\underline{\check{s}}}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{F},\beta)
denote the length of the longest sequence of contexts
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
such that for all
i
, there exists
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\displaystyle\|f_{i}(x_{i})-{f^{\star}}(x_{i})\|>\beta,\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j\neq i}\|f_{i}(x_{j})-{f^{\star}}(x_{j})\|^{2}\leq\beta^{2}.

We define the scale-sensitive star number as
{\mathfrak{\check{s}}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{F},\beta)\vcentcolon={}\sup_{\beta>\beta_{0}}{\mathfrak{\underline{\check{s}}}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{F},\beta_{0})
.

The next result provides a relation between the star number, disagreement coefficient and the eluder dimension.

Lemma 6

(
Foster et al. 2020
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{K}\}
is a uniform Glivenko-Cantelli class. For any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\gamma,\varepsilon>0
, we have
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})\leq\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\beta;{f^{\star}})
,

\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon,\gamma;f^{\star}\right)\leq 4(\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{F},\gamma;{f^{\star}}))^{2}
and

\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon,\gamma;f^{\star}\right)\leq 4\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\gamma;{f^{\star}})
.

The following two technical lemmas are useful in bounding the total number of queries made by our selective sampling and imitation learning algorithms. We first provide a technical result which bounds the number of times we can find a function
f^{\prime}
in a refinement
\mathcal{F}_{t}
of
\mathcal{F}
, such that
f^{\prime}
is sufficiently far away from
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
. This result is a variant of
Russo and Van Roy 2013
, and first appears in
Foster et al. 2020
.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\{x_{t},y_{t},Z_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
be sequence of tuples, where
x_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
and
Z_{t}\in\{0,1\}
. Fix any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
, and define the set
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\{f\in\mathcal{F}\mid\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}(f(x_{s})[y_{s}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})[y_{s}])^{2}\leq\beta^{2}\}
. Then, for any
\zeta>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\exists f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}:(f^{\prime}(x_{t})[y_{t}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})[y_{t}])\geq\zeta\}\leq\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\zeta^{2}}+1\right)\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}}).

Proof.

We first note that we can always remove a tuple
\{(x_{t},y_{t}),Z_{t}\}
whenever
Z_{t}=0
without any effect on the conclusion. Hence, we can assume
Z_{t}=1
for all
t\in[T]
without loss of generality. Then the rest of the proof essentialy follows from
Foster et al. 2020
. For completeness, we state the full proof here.

For simplicity of presentation, we say
(x_{t},y_{t})
is
\zeta
-independent of
(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t-1},y_{t-1})
if there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\lvert f(x_{t})[y_{t}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})[y_{t}]\rvert\geq\zeta
and
\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}(f(x_{s})[y_{s}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})[y_{s}])^{2}\leq\zeta^{2}
. Otherwise, we say
x
is
\zeta
-dependent. The proof consists of the following two claims.

First, we claim that for any
t\in[T]
, if there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}_{t}
such that
\lvert f(x_{t})[y_{t}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})[y_{t}]\rvert\geq\zeta
, then
x_{t}
is
\zeta
-dependent on at most
\beta^{2}/\zeta^{2}
disjoint sequences of
(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t-1},y_{t-1})
. To show this, let’s say
x_{t}
is
\zeta
-dependent on a particular subsequence
(x_{i_{1}},y_{i_{1}}),\dots,(x_{i_{k}},y_{i_{k}})
while
\lvert f(x)[y]-{f^{\star}}(x)[y]\rvert\geq\zeta
. Then it must holds that

\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Big(f(x_{i_{j}})[y_{i_{j}}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{i_{j}})[y_{i_{j}}]\Big)^{2}\geq\zeta^{2}.

If there are
M
such disjoint subsequence, then we can add them up and obtain the following:

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\Big(f(x_{s})[y_{s}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})[y_{s}]\Big)^{2}\geq M\zeta^{2}.

By the construction of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, the left-hand side above is at most
\beta^{2}
. Hence we conclude that
\beta^{2}\geq M\zeta^{2}
, which implies
M\leq\beta^{2}/\zeta^{2}
.

Second, we claim that for any
k
and any sequence
(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{k},y_{k})
, there exists
j\leq k
such that
x_{j}
is
\zeta
-dependent on at least
N\vcentcolon={}\lfloor k\,/\,\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}})\rfloor
disjoint subsequences of
(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{j-1},y_{j-1})
. This can be proved by construction. Let
B_{1},\dots,B_{N}
be
N
subsequences of
(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{k},y_{k})
and are initialized with
B_{i}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}
. Then we repeat the following process for
j=N+1,N+2\dots,k
.

\bullet

We first check if
x_{j}
is
\zeta
-dependent on
B_{i}
for all
i\in[N]
. If so, we are done.

\bullet

Otherwise, pick an arbitrary
i\in[N]
for which
x_{j}
is
\zeta
-independent of
B_{i}
and append
(x_{j},y_{j})
to
B_{i}
, i.e.,
B_{i}\leftarrow B_{i}\cup\{(x_{j},y_{j})\}
.

If we don’t reach any
j
while running the above process for which the first statement above is satisfied, we should end up with
\sum_{i=1}^{N}|B_{i}|=k\geq N\cdot\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}})
. We note that by construction
|B_{i}|\leq\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}})
and thus
|B_{i}|=\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}})
for all
i\in[N]
, which implies
x_{k}
must be
\zeta
-dependent on all
B_{i}
.

Finally, let
x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}}
be the subsequence where, for all
s\in[k]
, there exists
f\in\mathcal{F}_{i_{s}}
such that
\lvert f(x_{i_{s}})[y_{i_{s}}]-{f^{\star}}(x_{i_{s}})[y_{i_{s}}]\rvert\geq\zeta
. By our first claim we know each element of this subsequence is
\zeta
-dependent on at most
\beta^{2}/\zeta^{2}
disjoint subsequences. By the second claim, we know that there exists an element that is
\zeta
-dependent on at least
\lfloor k\,/\,\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}})\rfloor
disjoint subsequences. So we must have
\lfloor k\,/\,\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}})\rfloor\leq\beta^{2}/\zeta^{2}
. Hence,
k\leq(\beta^{2}/\zeta^{2}+1)\cdot\check{\mathfrak{E}}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}})
.
∎

The following is an extension of Lemma
7
that holds for the normed version of eluder dimension given in Definition
1
. The proof is essentially the same so we skip it for conciseness.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\{x_{t},Z_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
be sequence of tuples, where
x_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
and
Z_{t}\in\{0,1\}
. Fix any
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
, and define the set
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\{f\in\mathcal{F}\mid\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\|^{2}\leq\beta^{2}\}
. Then, for any
\zeta>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\exists f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}:\|f^{\prime}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\geq\zeta\}\leq\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\zeta^{2}}+1\right)\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\zeta;{f^{\star}}).

Appendix C
Selective Sampling: Learning from Single Expert

C.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Before delving into the proof, we recall the relevant notation. In Algorithm
1
,

\bullet

The label
y_{t}\sim\phi({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))
, where
\phi
denotes the link-function given in
(
2
)
.

\bullet

The function
\mathtt{SelectAction}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\vcentcolon={}\argmax_{k}\phi(f_{t}(x_{t}))[k]
.

\bullet

For any vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, the margin is given by the gap between the value at the largest and the second largest coordinate, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(v)=\phi(v)[k^{\star}]-\max_{k\neq k^{\star}}\phi(v)[k],

where
k^{\star}\in\argmax_{k\in[K]}\phi(v)[k]
.

\bullet

We also define
T_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}
to denote the number of samples within
T
rounds of interaction for which the margin w.r.t.
{f^{\star}}
is smaller than
\varepsilon
.

\bullet

We define the function
\mathrm{Gap}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times[K]\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{+}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Gap}(v,k)=\max_{k^{\prime}}\phi(v)[k^{\prime}]-\phi(v)[k],

(16)

to denote the gap between the largest and the
k
-th coordinate of
v
.

C.1.1
Supporting Technical Results

Lemma 9
.

For any
u
, and
k^{\prime}\neq\argmax_{k}\phi(u)[k]
,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(u)\leq\mathrm{Gap}(u,k^{\prime}).

Proof.

Let
k^{\star}=\argmax_{k}\phi(u)[k]
. By definition,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Gap}(u,k^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\phi(u)[k^{\star}]-\phi(u)[k^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\geq\phi(u)[k^{\star}]-\max_{k^{\prime}\neq k}\phi(u)[k^{\prime}]=\mathtt{Margin}(u).

∎

The following technical result establishes a certain favorable property for the function
{f^{\star}}
, whose proof follows from the regret bound of the online oracle used in Algorithm
1
.

Lemma 10
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
, the function
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
satisfies the following for all
t\leq T
:

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}Z_{s}\|{f^{\star}}(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}\vcentcolon={}\frac{4}{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{\ell_{\phi}}}(\mathcal{F};T)}+\frac{112}{\lambda^{2}}\log(4\log^{2}(T)/\delta)
.

Proof.

The desired result follows from an application of Lemma
5
, where we note that we do not query oracle when
Z_{s}=0
, and thus do not count the time steps for which
Z_{s}=0
.
∎

Throughout the proof, we condition on the
1-\delta
probability event that Lemma
10
holds. The next technical lemma allows us to bound the number of times when we query for the label and
\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\zeta
in terms of the eluder dimension (normed version) of the function class
\mathcal{F}
. Note that Lemma
11
holds even if the sequence
\{x_{t}\}_{t\leq T}
could be adversarially generated.

Lemma 11
.

Let
{f^{\star}}
satisfy Lemma
10
, and let
\Delta_{t}(x_{t})
be defined in
(
5
)
in Algorithm
1
. Then, for any
\zeta>0
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\nicefrac{{\zeta}}{{2}};{f^{\star}})\right).

where
\mathfrak{E}
denotes the eluder dimension is given in Definition
1
.

Proof.

Let
f^{\star}_{t}
denote the maximizer of
(
5
)
at round
t
on point
x_{t}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Delta_{t}(x_{t})=\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}\left\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}.

(17)

However, recall that Lemma
10
implies that, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the function
{f^{\star}}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}\left\|{f^{\star}}(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}.

(18)

Using
(
17
)
,
(
18
)
and Triangle inequality, we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}\left\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}\left\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-f_{s}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}+2\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}\left\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{s}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}.

(19)

Next, note that, an application of Triangle inequality implies that
\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|+\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|
. Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|\geq\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|+\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|\geq\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\geq\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\geq\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\geq\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}+\frac{4}{\zeta^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}(f_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\geq\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}+\frac{4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}}{\zeta^{2}},

(20)

where in the last line we used Lemma
10
to bound the second term. In the following, we show how to bound the first term. Recall that for any
t\leq T
, the function
f^{\star}_{t}
satisfies
(
19
)
. Thus, we wish to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\geq\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Z_{s}(f^{\star}_{t}(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)},

for all
t\leq T
. An application of Lemma
8
in the above implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\|f^{\star}_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\geq\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{17{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\nicefrac{{\zeta}}{{2}};{f^{\star}}).

(21)

where in the last line, we used the fact that
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}}}{{\zeta^{2}}}\geq 1
, for our parameter setting.

Plugging in the bound
(
21
)
in
(
20
)
, and using the fact that
\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\nicefrac{{\zeta}}{{2}};{f^{\star}})\geq 1
, we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{20{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\nicefrac{{\zeta}}{{2}};{f^{\star}}).

∎

The next two technical lemma’s relate the margin to the gap between functions, and are useful in the analysis for regret / total number of queries.

Lemma 12
.

Suppose the functions
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are defined such that
\pi_{i}(x)=\argmax_{k\in[K]}\phi(f_{i}(x))[k]
. Then, for any
x
for which
\pi_{1}(x)\neq\pi_{2}(x)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(f_{1}(x))\leq\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{1}(x)]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{2}(x)]\leq 2\gamma\|f_{1}(x)-f_{2}(x)\|_{2},

where
\gamma
-denotes the Lipschitz parameter of the link function
\phi
.

Proof.

First note

\phi(f_{2}(x))[\pi_{2}(x)]\geq\phi(f_{2}(x))[\pi_{1}(x)]
by the definition of
\pi_{2}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{1}(x)]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{2}(x)]

\displaystyle\leq\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{1}(x)]-\phi(f_{2}(x))[\pi_{1}(x)]+\phi(f_{2}(x))[\pi_{2}(x)]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{2}(x)]

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\phi(f_{1}(x))-\phi(f_{2}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\phi(f_{1}(x))-\phi(f_{2}(x))\|_{2}.

Using the fact that
\phi
is
\gamma
-Lipschitz, we immediately get that

\displaystyle\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{1}(x)]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[\pi_{2}(x)]

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\|f_{1}(x)-f_{2}(x)\|_{2}.

∎

Lemma 13
.

For any two function
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
, and
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(f_{1}(x))-\mathtt{Margin}(f_{2}(x))

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\|f_{1}(x)-f_{2}(x)\|.

Proof.

For the ease of notation, define

\displaystyle k_{1}=\argmax_{k\in[k]}\phi(f_{1}(x))[k]\qquad\text{and}\qquad k^{\prime}_{1}=\argmax_{k^{\prime}\neq k_{1}}\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}],

where ties are broken arbitrarily but consistently. Similarly, we define

\displaystyle k_{2}=\argmax_{k\in[k]}\phi(f_{2}(x))[k]\qquad\text{and}\qquad k^{\prime}_{2}=\argmax_{k^{\prime}\neq k_{2}}\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}].

(22)

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(f_{1}(x))=\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{1}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}],

and

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(f_{2}(x))=\phi(f_{2}(x))[k_{2}]-\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}].

(23)

Finally, also note that for any coordinate
k
,

\displaystyle\phi(f_{1}(x))[k]-\phi(f_{2}(x))[k]\leq\|\phi(f_{2}(x))-\phi(f_{1}(x))\|.

(24)

We now proceed with the proof. Plugging in the form in
(
23
)
, we get that

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\mathtt{Margin}(f_{1}(x))-\mathtt{Margin}(f_{2}(x))

\displaystyle=\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{1}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}]-\left(\phi(f_{2}(x))[k_{2}]-\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{1}]-\phi(f_{2}(x))[k_{2}]\right)+\left(\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{1}]-\phi(f_{2}(x))[k_{1}]\right)+\left(\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(f_{2}(x))-\phi(f_{1}(x))\|+(\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}]),

where the first inequality uses the fact that
k_{2}
is the maximizer coordinate of
\phi(f_{2}(x))
and the last inequality uses
(
24
)
. In the following, we bound the second term in the right hand side above under the following three cases:

\bullet

Case 1:
k^{\prime}_{2}\neq k_{1}
:
Since
k^{\prime}_{2}\neq k_{1}
, we note that replacing
k_{1}^{\prime}
by
k^{\prime}_{2}
in the second term will only increase the value (see the definition in
(
22
)
). Thus,

\displaystyle\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}]

\displaystyle\leq\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(f_{2}(x))-\phi(f_{1}(x))\|,

where the last line uses
(
24
)
.

\bullet

Case 2a:
k^{\prime}_{2}=k_{1},k_{2}=k^{\prime}_{1}
:
Using definition of
k_{2}
in
(
22
)
, we note that

\displaystyle\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}]

\displaystyle=\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\phi(f_{2}(x))[k_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(f_{2}(x))-\phi(f_{1}(x))\|,

where the last line uses
(
24
)
.

\bullet

Case 2b:
k^{\prime}_{2}=k_{1},k_{2}\neq k^{\prime}_{1}
:
Using the fact that
k^{\prime}_{2}=k_{1}
and that
k_{2}\neq k^{\prime}_{2}
, we get that
k_{2}\neq k_{1}
. Thus using the definition of
k_{1}^{\prime}
along with the fact that
k_{2}\neq k_{1}
, we get that

\displaystyle\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k^{\prime}_{1}]

\displaystyle\leq\phi(f_{2}(x))[k^{\prime}_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\phi(f_{2}(x))[k_{2}]-\phi(f_{1}(x))[k_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(f_{2}(x))-\phi(f_{1}(x))\|,

where the second last line uses definition of
k_{2}
and the last line uses
(
24
)
.

Combining all the above bounds together implies that

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(f_{1}(x))-\mathtt{Margin}(f_{2}(x))

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\phi(f_{2}(x))-\phi(f_{1}(x))\|.

The final statement follows since
\phi
is
\gamma
-Lipschitz.
∎

C.1.2
Regret Bound

For the ease of notation, for the rest of the proof in this section we define the function
{\pi^{\star}}
such that

\displaystyle{\pi^{\star}}(x)=\argmax_{k}\phi({f^{\star}}(x))[k].

Additionally, we recall that for any time
t
,
\widehat{y}_{t}=\mathtt{SelectAction}(f_{t}(x_{t}))=\argmax_{k}\phi(f_{t}(x))[k]
. Starting from the definition of the regret, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Pr\left(\widehat{y}_{t}\neq y_{t}\right)-\Pr\left({\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\neq y_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\left\lvert\Pr\left(y_{t}={\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\right)-\Pr\left(y_{t}=\widehat{y}_{t}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\left\lvert\phi({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))[{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})]-\phi({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))[\widehat{y}_{t}]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t}),

where the second last line uses the probabilistic model from which labels are generated, and the last inequality plugs in the definition of
\mathrm{Gap}
from
(
59
)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be a free parameter. We can decompose the above regret bound further as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})

Using the fact that
\mathsf{y}_{t}(x_{t})=\argmax_{k\in[K]}\phi(f_{t}(x))[k]
along with the definition of
\mathrm{Gap}
and Lemma
12
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{Z}_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

(25)

\displaystyle=T_{\varepsilon}\cdot\varepsilon+2\gamma\cdot T_{A}+2\gamma\cdot T_{B}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|,

where the second inequality holds because
\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})\leq\varepsilon
implies that
\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon
whenever
\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})
. In the last line above, we plugged in the definition of
T_{\varepsilon}
, and defined
\mathtt{T}_{A}
and
\mathtt{T}_{B}
as the second term and the last term respectively (upto constants). We bound them separately below:

\bullet

Bound on
\mathtt{T}_{A}
:
We note that

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|>\varepsilon/2\gamma\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

where the second line follows from Lemma
12
and because
\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})
.
Using the fact that
\mathbf{1}\{a\geq b\}\leq\nicefrac{{a}}{{b}}
for all
a,b\geq 0
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{A}\leq 4\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\frac{\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|^{2}}{\varepsilon}.

(26)

\bullet

Bound on
\mathtt{T}_{B}
:
Fix any
t\leq T
, and note that Lemma
10
implies that
\sum_{s=1}^{t}\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}
. Thus
{f^{\star}}
satisfies the constraint in the definition of
\Delta_{t}
in
(
5
)
and we must have that

\displaystyle\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{t}(x_{t}).

(27)

Plugging in the definition of
Z_{t}
, we note that

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))>2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\|f_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|>\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\},

where the second inequality is due Lemma
12
.
However, note that the term inside the indicator contradicts
(
27
)
(which always holds). Thus,

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{B}

\displaystyle=0.

(28)

Combining the bounds
(
26
)
and
(
28
)
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon T_{\varepsilon}+8\gamma^{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\frac{\|f_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|^{2}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon T_{\varepsilon}+\frac{8\gamma^{2}}{\varepsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)},

where the last inequality is due to Lemma
10
.

Since
\varepsilon
is a free parameter above, the final bound follows by choosing the best parameter
\varepsilon
, and by plugging in the form of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}
.

C.1.3
Total Number of Queries

We use the notation
N_{T}
to denote the total number of expert queries made by the learner within
T
rounds of interactions. Let
\varepsilon>0
be a free parameter. Using the definition of
Z_{t}
, we have that

\displaystyle N_{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon,\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\leq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon,\Delta_{t}(x_{t})>\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}}\}

(29)

\displaystyle=T_{\varepsilon}+\mathtt{T}_{D}+\mathtt{T}_{E},

where in the last line we use the definition of
T_{\varepsilon}
, and defined
\mathtt{T}_{D}
and
\mathtt{T}_{E}
respectively. We bound them separately below:

\bullet

Bound on
\mathtt{T}_{D}
.

Recall
(
27
)
which implies that
{f^{\star}}
satisfies the bound
\|f_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{t}(x_{t})
. Thus, using Lemma
13
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\|f_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|+t\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t})+\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t})).

The above implies that

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{D}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon,\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\leq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq 4\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon,\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\leq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}}\}

\displaystyle\leq 0,

where the last line follows from the fact that all the conditions inside the indictor can not hold simultaneously.

\bullet

Bound on
\mathtt{T}_{E}
.
We note that

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{E}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon,\Delta_{t}(x_{t})>\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{320{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}};{f^{\star}}).

where the last line follows from setting
\zeta=\varepsilon/4\gamma
in Lemma
11
.

Gathering the bounds above, we get that

\displaystyle N_{T}

\displaystyle\leq T_{\varepsilon}+\frac{640\gamma^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\mathfrak{E}(\mathcal{F},\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4\gamma}};{f^{\star}}).

Since
\varepsilon
is a free parameter above, the final bound follows by choosing the best parameter
\varepsilon
, and by plugging in the form of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}
.

C.2
Proof of Theorem
2

1:

Parameters
\delta,\gamma,\lambda,T
, function class
\mathcal{F}
, and online regression oracle
\mathsf{Oracle}
w.r.t
\ell_{\phi}
.

2:

Set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}=\frac{4}{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{\ell_{\phi}}}(\mathcal{F};T)}+\frac{112}{\lambda^{2}}\log(4\log^{2}(T)/\delta)
, Compute
f_{1}\leftarrow\text{$\mathsf{Oracle}$}_{1}(\emptyset)
.

3:

Set
E=\left\lceil\log(T)\right\rceil
and
\tau_{e}=2^{e-1}
for
e\leq E
.

4:

for

e=1,\dots,E-1

do

5:

Learner constructs the feasible set of optimal functions
\mathcal{F}_{e}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{e}=\left\{f\in\mathcal{F}\mid\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}\right\}.

(30)

6:

for

t

\leftarrow\tau_{e}

to

\tau_{e+1}-1

do

7:

Nature samples
x_{t}
from an (unknown) distribution
\mu
.

8:

Learner computes

\displaystyle g_{t}\in\argmin_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\penalty\ \mathtt{Margin}(g(x_{t})),\quad\text{and,}\quad\Delta_{e}(x_{t})\vcentcolon={}\max_{f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\penalty\ \|f(x_{t})-f^{\prime}(x_{t})\|.

(31)

9:

Learner decides whether to query:
Z_{t}=\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(g_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{e}(x_{t})\}
.

10:

if

Z_{t}=1

then

11:

Learner plays the action
\widehat{y}_{t}=\mathtt{SelectAction}(f_{t}(x_{t}))
.

12:

Learner queries the label
y_{t}
on
x_{t}
.

13:

f_{t+1}\leftarrow\text{$\mathsf{Oracle}$}_{t}(\{x_{t},y_{t}\})
.

14:

else

15:

Learner plays the action
\widehat{y}_{t}=\mathtt{SelectAction}(g_{t}(x_{t}))
.

16:

f_{t+1}\leftarrow f_{t}
.

Algorithm 4

Selective Sampling with Expert Feedback for Stochastic Contexts

Before delving into the proof, we recall the relevant notation. In Algorithm
4
,

\bullet

The label
y_{t}\sim\phi({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))
, where
\phi
denotes the link-function given in
(
2
)
.

\bullet

The function
\mathtt{SelectAction}(f_{t}(x_{t}))\vcentcolon={}\argmax_{k}\phi(f_{t}(x_{t}))[k]
.

\bullet

For any vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, the margin is given by the gap between the value at the largest and the second largest coordinate, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathtt{Margin}(v)=\phi(v)[k^{\star}]-\max_{k\neq k^{\star}}\phi(v)[k],

where
k^{\star}\in\argmax_{k\in[K]}\phi(v)[k]
.

\bullet

We also define
T_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}
to denote the number of samples within
T
rounds of interaction for which the margin w.r.t.
{f^{\star}}
is smaller than
\varepsilon
.

\bullet

We define the function
\mathrm{Gap}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times[K]\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{+}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Gap}(v,k)=\max_{k^{\prime}}\phi(v)[k^{\prime}]-\phi(v)[k],

(32)

to denote the gap between the largest and the
k
-th coordinate of
v
. Recall that Lemma
9
holds.

\bullet

Additionally, we define the function
Z^{e}
to denote the query condition

\displaystyle Z^{e}(x)=\mathbf{1}\{\inf_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\mathtt{Margin}(g(x))\leq 2\gamma\sup_{f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)\|\}.

(33)

The definition in
(
33
)
suggests that for all
t\in[\tau_{e},\tau_{e+1})
,
Z_{t}=Z^{e}(x_{t})
, for all
e\leq E-1
.

Intuition for epoching.

We next provide intuition on why epoching in needed in Algorithm
4
to get the improved query complexity bound. From the proof sketch in Section
3
, the term
\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\varepsilon\}
appearing in the query complexity bound is handled using the eluder dimension of
\mathcal{F}
. When
x_{t}
is sampled i.i.d. we wish to bound this using disagreement-coefficient instead. However, note that in Algorithm
1
the query condition
Z_{t}
depends on the samples
\{x_{s}\}_{s<t}
drawn in all previous time steps and the corresponding query conditions
\{Z_{s}\}_{s<t}
. This introduces a bias, and thus the terms
Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\varepsilon\}
are no longer independent to each other. Thus, we can not directly used distributional properties like the disagreement coefficient to bound the query complexity. Algorithm
4
fixes this issue by defining epochs of doubling length such that the query condition in epoch
e
only depends on the samples presented to the learner at time steps before this epoch (i.e. in time steps
1\leq t\leq\tau_{e}-1
. Thus, the terms
Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\geq\varepsilon\}
for
\tau_{e}\leq t<\tau_{e+1}
are i.i.d. allowing us to get bounds in terms of distributional properties like the disagreement coefficient of
\mathcal{F}
.

However, note that whenever we query in Algorithm
4
, we still choose the labels according to the estimate from the online regression oracle and thus the regret bound remains unchanged.

C.2.1
Supporting Technical Results

The following lemma establishes useful technical properties of the function
{f^{\star}}
and the sets
\mathcal{F}_{e}
.

Lemma 14
.

Suppose Algorithm
4
is run on the sequence
\{x_{t}\}_{t\leq T}
drawn i.i.d. from the unknown distribution
\mu
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, each of the following holds:

(a)

For all
t\leq T
, the function
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}Z_{s}\|{f^{\star}}(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}=\frac{4}{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{\ell_{\phi}}}(\mathcal{F};T)}+\frac{112}{\lambda^{2}}\log(4\log^{2}(T)/\delta)
.

Thus,
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
for all
e\leq E-1
, and
\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-g_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{e}(x_{t})
for all
\tau_{e}\leq t\leq\tau_{e+1}-1
.

(b)

For any function
f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
, we have

\displaystyle\En\left[\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)},

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}\vcentcolon={}2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}+4c_{2}\left(\log^{4}(T)\sup_{\tau\leq T}\left(\tau\mathsf{Rad}^{2}_{\tau}(\mathcal{F})\right)+2\log(T)\log(E/\delta)\right)
.

(c)

For any
e\leq E
, and any function
f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\bar{\nu}_{e}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\tau_{e}-1}}

where the sub-distributions
\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}(x)\vcentcolon={}Z^{\bar{e}}(x)\mu(x)
and
\bar{\nu}_{e}\vcentcolon={}\frac{1}{\tau_{e}-\tau_{1}}\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}(\tau_{{\bar{e}}+1}-\tau_{\bar{e}})\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}
.

(d)

For any
{\bar{e}}<e
, the corresponding sets
\mathcal{F}_{e}
and
\mathcal{F}_{{\bar{e}}}
satisfy the relation
\mathcal{F}_{e}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{{\bar{e}}}
.

(e)

For any
{\bar{e}}\leq e
, we have
\bar{\mu}_{e}\preccurlyeq\bar{\mu}_{{\bar{e}}}
.

Proof.

We prove each part separately below:

(a)

An application of Lemma
5
, where we note that we do not query oracle when
Z_{s}=0
, and thus do not count the time steps for which
Z_{s}=0
, implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}Z_{s}\|{f^{\star}}(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\|^{2}\leq\frac{4}{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{\ell_{\phi}}}(\mathcal{F};T)}+\frac{112}{\lambda^{2}}\log(4\log^{2}(T)/\delta)=\vcentcolon{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}

for all
t\leq T
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Using the above for
t=\tau_{e+1}-1
implies that
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
for all
e\leq E-1
. Since, we also have that
g_{t}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
(by construction) for all
\tau_{e}\leq t\leq\tau_{e+1}-1
, plugging in the definition of
\Delta_{e}(x_{t})
, we immediately get that
\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-g_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{e}(x_{t})
.

(b)

Fix any epoch number
{\bar{e}}\leq E-1
, and consider the time steps
\tau_{\bar{e}}\leq t<\tau_{{\bar{e}}+1}
. Define the loss function

\displaystyle\ell_{\bar{e}}(f(x),{f^{\star}}(x))=Z^{\bar{e}}(x)\left\|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\right\|^{2}

where
Z^{\bar{e}}
denotes the query conditions at epoch
{\bar{e}}
(defined in
(
33
)
), and recall that
Z^{\bar{e}}
does not depend on any samples that are drawn at epoch
{\bar{e}}
(by definition). Furthermore, note that
\ell_{\bar{e}}
is
2
-smooth w.r.t.
f
and satisfies
\ell_{\bar{e}}(f(x),{f^{\star}}(x))\leq 4
for all
f,{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
and
x
. Thus using Lemma
2
, we get that for any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
, with probability at least
1-\delta/E
,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt\En\left[\sum_{s=\tau_{\bar{e}}}^{\tau_{{\bar{e}}+1}-1}Z^{\bar{e}}(x_{s})\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{s=\tau_{\bar{e}}}^{\tau_{{\bar{e}}+1}-1}Z^{\bar{e}}(x_{s})\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}+c_{2}\left(2{\tau_{e}}\log^{3}(\tau_{e})\mathsf{Rad}^{2}_{\tau_{e}}(\mathcal{F}_{e})+4\log(E/\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{s=\tau_{\bar{e}}}^{\tau_{{\bar{e}}+1}-1}Z^{\bar{e}}(x_{s})\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}+2c_{2}\left(\log^{3}(T)\sup_{\tau\leq T}\left(\tau\mathsf{Rad}^{2}_{\tau}(\mathcal{F})\right)+2\log(E/\delta)\right),

where in the last line we used the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{e}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
. Summing the above for all
\bar{e}\leq e-1
, we get that for any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt\En\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z^{e}(x_{s})\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z^{e}(x_{s})\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}+4c_{2}E\left(\log^{3}(T)\sup_{\tau\leq T}\left(\tau\mathsf{Rad}^{2}_{\tau}(\mathcal{F})\right)+2\log(E/\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}+4c_{2}\left(\log^{4}(T)\sup_{\tau\leq T}\left(\tau\mathsf{Rad}^{2}_{\tau}(\mathcal{F})\right)+2\log(T)\log(E/\delta)\right),

where the last line follows by using the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{e}
, and that
E\leq\lceil\log(T)\rceil
, and that
\mathcal{F}_{e}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.

(c)

Starting from part-

(
b
)

, we first note that

\displaystyle\En\left[\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}.

(34)

Additionally, also note that

\displaystyle\En\left[\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\En\left[\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}\sum_{s=\tau_{\bar{e}}}^{\tau_{\bar{e}+1}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\En\left[\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}\sum_{s=\tau_{\bar{e}}}^{\tau_{\bar{e}+1}-1}Z^{\bar{e}}(x_{s})\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}\sum_{s=\tau_{\bar{e}}}^{\tau_{\bar{e}+1}-1}\En_{x_{s}\sim\mu}\left[Z^{\bar{e}}(x_{s})\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}\left(\tau_{\bar{e}+1}-\tau_{\bar{e}}\right)\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[Z^{\bar{e}}(x)\left\|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\right\|^{2}\right],

where the last two lines use the fact that the query condition
Z^{\bar{e}}
does not depend on the samples from rounds
t=\tau_{\bar{e}}
to
\tau_{\bar{e}+1}-1
. Plugging in the definition of
\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}
in the above, we get that

\displaystyle\En\left[\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}\left(\tau_{\bar{e}+1}-\tau_{\bar{e}}\right)\cdot\En_{x\sim\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}}\left[\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=(\tau_{e}-\tau_{1})\cdot\En_{x\sim\bar{\nu}_{e}}\left[\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=(\tau_{e}-\tau_{1})\cdot\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}_{\bar{\nu}_{e}},

(35)

where in the second line, we used the fact that the sub-distribution
\bar{\nu}_{e}\vcentcolon={}\frac{1}{\tau_{e}-\tau_{1}}\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}(\tau_{{\bar{e}}+1}-\tau_{\bar{e}})\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}
.

Combining
(
34
)
and
(
35
)
, we get that

\displaystyle\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\bar{\nu}_{e}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\tau_{e}-1}}

(d)

The argument follows from the definition of the set
\mathcal{F}_{e}
as any function
f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
that satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{e}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)},

also satisfies the constraint

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{\bar{e}}-1}Z_{s}\left\|f(x_{s})-f_{s}(x_{s})\right\|^{2}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)},

for any
\bar{e}\leq e
, since the left hand side consists of lesser number of terms and all terms are non-negative. Thus,
\mathcal{F}_{e}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\bar{e}}
.

(e)

Recall that for any
e\leq E-1
, the sub-probability measure
\bar{\mu}_{e}(x)\vcentcolon={}Z^{e}(x)\mu(x)
where
Z^{e}(x)=\mathbf{1}\{\min_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|g(x)\|\leq\Delta_{e}(x)\}
, and
\Delta_{e}(x)=\max_{f^{\prime},f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\penalty\ \|f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)\|
. First note that for any
\bar{e}\leq e
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}(x)=\max_{f^{\prime},f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\penalty\ \|f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)\|\leq\max_{f^{\prime},f\in\mathcal{F}_{\bar{e}}}\penalty\ \|f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)\|=\Delta_{\bar{e}}(x),

where the inequality above holds because
\mathcal{F}_{e}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{{\bar{e}}}
due to part-(d) above. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\min_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|g(x)\|

\displaystyle\geq\min_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{{\bar{e}}}}\|g(x)\|,

again because
\mathcal{F}_{e}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{{\bar{e}}}
. Thus,

\displaystyle Z^{e}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}\{\min_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|g(x)\|\leq\Delta_{e}(x)\}\leq\mathbf{1}\{\min_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{\bar{e}}}\|g(x)\|\leq\Delta_{\bar{e}}(x)\}\leq Z^{\bar{e}}(x).

The above implies that
\bar{\mu}_{e}\leq\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}
.

∎

Lemma 15
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0},\gamma_{0}\geq 0
, and
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
. Then, for any sub distribution
\bar{\mu}
such that
\En_{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[\mathbf{1}\{x\in\mathcal{X}\}\right]>0
,
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{0}
and
\gamma\geq\gamma_{0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\Pr_{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\varepsilon,\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\gamma\right)\leq\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon_{0},\gamma_{0};{f^{\star}}\right).

Proof.

The key idea in the proof is to go from sub distributions to distributions, and then invoking the definition of
\theta
from Definition
2
. Define
\kappa=\En_{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left[\mathbf{1}\{x\in\mathcal{X}\}\right]
. Since
0<\kappa\leq 1
, we can define a probability measure
\mu
such that
\mu(x)=\nicefrac{{\bar{\mu}(x)}}{{\kappa}}
. Thus, for any
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{0}
and
\gamma\geq\gamma_{0}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\Pr_{x\sim\bar{\mu}}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\varepsilon,\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\gamma\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\nicefrac{{\gamma^{2}}}{{k}}}\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\varepsilon,\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\bar{\mu}}\leq\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\nicefrac{{\gamma^{2}}}{{k}}}\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\varepsilon,\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\mu}\leq\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{\sqrt{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\bar{\gamma}^{2}}\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\varepsilon,\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\mu}\leq\bar{\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{0},\bar{\gamma}\geq\gamma_{0}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\bar{\gamma}^{2}}\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\left(\exists f\in\mathcal{F}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\varepsilon,\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\mu}\leq\bar{\gamma}\right),

where in the second last line, we defined
\bar{\gamma}=\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{\sqrt{k}}}
, and the last line used the fact that both
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{0}
and
\bar{\gamma}\geq\gamma_{0}
. The final statement follows by noting the fact that
\mu
is a distribution and the definition of the disagreement coefficient
\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(;\right)
from Definition
2
.
∎

The following technical result will be useful in bounding the query complexity for Algorithm
4
.

Lemma 16
.

For any
t\leq T
, let
e(t)
denotes the epoch number such that
\tau_{e(t)}\leq t<\tau_{e(t)+1}
. Let
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}
satisfy Lemma
14
, and let
\Delta_{e(t)}(x_{t})
be defined in Algorithm
4
. Then, for any
\zeta>0
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e(t)}(x_{t})\geq\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 12\log(T)\cdot\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{T};f^{\star}\right)+4\log(2/\delta).

Proof.

Recall the definition of the query rule
Z^{e}
given in
(
33
)
, and note that the function
Z^{e}
is independent of the samples
\{x_{t}\}_{t=\tau_{e}}^{\tau_{e+1}-1}
chosen by the nature for time steps at epoch
e
. Additionally, also recall that at every time step,
x_{t}
is sampled independently from the distribution
\mu
. Thus, using the query condition
Z^{e}
, we can define the sub-probability measure

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{e}\vcentcolon={}\mu(x)Z^{e}(x),

(36)

such that
\bar{\mu}_{e}(x)=\mu(x)
whenever
Z_{e}(x)=1
and is
0
otherwise. Furthermore, for any
e\in E-1
, we define the sub-probability measure
\bar{\nu}_{e}
as

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{e}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau_{e}-\tau_{1}}\sum_{\bar{e}=1}^{e-1}(\tau_{{\bar{e}}+1}-\tau_{{\bar{e}}})\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}.

(37)

We now move to the main proof. First fix any epoch
e\leq E-1
, and consider any round
t\in[\tau_{e},\tau_{e+1}-1]
. Using the definition of
\Delta_{e}(x_{t})
and definition of
Z^{e}
from
(
33
)
in the above, we get that

\displaystyle\En_{x_{t}\sim\mu}\left[Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[Z^{e}(x_{t})\mathbf{1}\left\{\sup_{f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x_{t})-f^{\prime}(x_{t})\|>\zeta\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[Z^{e}(x_{t})\mathbf{1}\left\{\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|>\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}\right]

where the second line follows because
{f^{\star}}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
, and because
\sup_{f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x_{t})-f^{\prime}(x_{t})\|\leq 2\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|
due to Triangle inequality. Plugging in the definition of
\bar{\mu}_{e}
from
(
36
)
in the above we get that

\displaystyle\En_{x_{t}\sim\mu}\left[Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\bar{\mu}_{e}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|>\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\bar{\mu}_{\bar{e}}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|>\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}\right].

(38)

for all
\bar{e}\leq e
, where the last inequality follows from Lemma
14
-

(
e
)

. Since the above holds for all
\bar{e}\leq e
, we immediately get that

\displaystyle\En_{x_{t}\sim\mu}\left[Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\bar{\nu}_{\bar{e}}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}}\|f(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|>\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\bar{\nu}_{\bar{e}}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{\exists f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\frac{\zeta}{2}\right\}\right],

(39)

where the sub-probability measure
\bar{\nu}_{\bar{e}}
is defined in
(
37
)
. Additionally, recall that Lemma
14
-

(
b
)

implies that with probability at least
1-\delta
any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\bar{\nu}_{e}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\tau_{e}-1}}.

(40)

Conditioning on the above event, and plugging it in
(
39
)
, we get that

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt\En_{x_{t}\sim\mu}\left[Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\bar{\nu}_{\bar{e}}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{\exists f\in\mathcal{F}_{e}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f(x)-{f^{\star}}(x)\|>\frac{\zeta}{2},\left\|f(x_{s})-{f^{\star}}(x_{s})\right\|_{\bar{\nu}_{e}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\tau_{e}-1}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{(\tau_{e}-1)\zeta^{2}}\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\tau_{e}-1};f^{\star}\right),

(41)

where the last inequality uses Lemma
15
.

Summing up the bound in
(
41
)
for each term
t=1
to
T
, we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En_{x_{t}}\left[Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e=1}^{E-1}\sum_{t=\tau_{e}}^{\tau_{e+1}-1}\En_{x_{t}}\left[Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum_{e=1}^{E-1}\left(\tau_{e+1}-\tau_{e}\right)\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{(\tau_{e}-1)\zeta^{2}}\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\tau_{e}-1};f^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 8\sum_{e=1}^{E-1}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\tau_{e}-1};f^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 8\sum_{e=1}^{E-1}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{T};f^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 8\log(T)\cdot\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{T};f^{\star}\right)

where the second inequality uses the fact that
\tau_{e+1}=2\tau_{e}
and that
\tau_{1}=1
, the third inequality holds due to monotonicity of
\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\cdot;f^{\star}\right)
and the last line simply plugs in the value of
E=\log(T)
.

Using Lemma
4
with the above bound for the sequence of random variable
X_{t}=Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}
, we get that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En_{x_{t}}\left[Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\left\{\Delta_{e}(x_{t})>\zeta\right\}\right]+4\log(2/\delta)

\displaystyle\leq 12\log(T)\cdot\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{\zeta^{2}}\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\left(\mathcal{F},\frac{\zeta}{2},\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{\Psi}^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F};T)}}{T};f^{\star}\right)+4\log(2/\delta).

The final result follows by taking a union bound of the above and the event in
(
40
)
.
∎

C.2.2
Regret Bound

For the ease of notation, through the proofs in this section we define the operators
{\pi^{\star}}
as

\displaystyle{\pi^{\star}}(x)=\argmax_{k}\phi({f^{\star}}(x))[k].

Furthermore, recall that
\widehat{y}_{t}
denotes the action chosen by the learner at round
t
of interaction. Starting from the definition of the regret, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Pr\left(\widehat{y}_{t}\neq y_{t}\right)-\Pr\left({\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\neq y_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\left\lvert\Pr\left(y_{t}={\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\right)-\Pr\left(y_{t}=\widehat{y}_{t}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\left\lvert\phi({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))[{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})]-\phi({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))[\widehat{y}_{t}]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t}),

where the last inequality plugs in the definition of
\mathrm{Gap}
from
(
59
)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be a free parameter. We can decompose the above regret bound further as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})

Using the fact that
\mathsf{y}_{t}(x_{t})=\argmax_{k\in[K]}\phi(f_{t}(x))[k]
in the above along with the definition of
\mathrm{Gap}
and Lemma
12
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}\cdot\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{Z}_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle=T_{\varepsilon}\cdot\varepsilon+2\gamma\cdot T_{A}+2\gamma\cdot T_{B}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|,

where the second inequality holds because
\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})\leq\varepsilon
implies that
\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon
whenever
\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})
. In the last line we plugged in the definition of
T_{\varepsilon}
, and defined
\mathtt{T}_{A}
and
\mathtt{T}_{B}
as the second term and the last term respectively. We bound term separately below:

\bullet

Bound on
\mathtt{T}_{A}
:
Note that whenever
Z_{t}=1
, we choose
\widehat{y}_{t}=\argmax_{k}\phi(f_{t}(x_{t}))[k]
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t}),\mathrm{Gap}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t})>\varepsilon\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\mathbf{1}\{\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|>\varepsilon/2\gamma\}\cdot\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|

where the second line follows from Lemma
12
and because
\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})
.
Using the fact that
\mathbf{1}\{a\geq b\}\leq\nicefrac{{a}}{{b}}
for all
a,b\geq 0
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{A}\leq 4\gamma\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\frac{\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})\|^{2}}{\varepsilon}.

(42)

\bullet

Bound on
\mathtt{T}_{B}
:
Fix any
t\leq T
, and let
e
be such that
\tau_{e}\leq t<\tau_{e+1}
. Next, note that from
Lemma
14
, we have

\displaystyle\|{f^{\star}}(x_{t})-g_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{e}(x_{t}).

(43)

Plugging in the definition of
Z_{t}
, we note that

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(g_{t}(x_{t}))>2\gamma\Delta_{e}(x_{t}),\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\|g_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|>\Delta_{e}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon\},

where the second inequality is due Lemma
12
and by noting that
\widehat{y}_{t}\neq{\pi^{\star}}(x_{t})
and that when
Z_{t}=0
, we choose
\widehat{y}_{t}=\argmax_{k}\phi(g_{t}(x_{t}))[k]
.
However, note that the term inside the indicator contradicts
(
43
)
(which always holds). Thus,

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}_{B}

\displaystyle=0.

(44)

Combining the bounds
(
42
)
and
(
44
)
, we get that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon T_{\varepsilon}+8\gamma^{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\frac{\|f_{t}(x_{t})-{f^{\star}}(x_{t})\|^{2}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon T_{\varepsilon}+\frac{8\gamma^{2}}{\varepsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)},

where the last inequality is due to Lemma
14
.

Since
\varepsilon
is a free parameter above, the final bound follows by choosing the best parameter
\varepsilon
, and by plugging in the form of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Psi^{\ell_{\phi}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{F},T)}
.

C.2.3
Total Number of Queries

Let
N_{T}
denote the total number of expert queries made by the learner within
T
rounds of interactions. For the ease of notation, define
\Delta_{t}(x_{t})=\Delta_{e(t)}(x_{t})
where
e(t)
denotes the epoch number for which
\tau_{e(t)}\leq t<\tau_{e(t)+1}
. Additionally, let
\varepsilon>0
be a free parameter. Thus,

\displaystyle N_{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(g_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t})\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(g_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))\leq\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\mathtt{Margin}(g_{t}(x_{t}))\leq 2\gamma\Delta_{t}(x_{t}),\mathtt{Margin}({f^{\star}}(x_{t}))>\varepsilon\}
